#7012. The 30 Best Halloween TV Episodes of All Time

There is no shortage of sitcoms, superheroes, or Snoopy to binge before trick-or-treating.

Fall TV died a slow and painful death many years ago. But that doesn’t mean we’ve all lost the plot on Halloween. Even in the streaming era, television shows still know when to drop the occasional Halloween episode, wherein familiar fictional characters dress up as other familiar fictional characters. So now is a great time to throw on a few favorites while you put together your last-minute costume. (You do have a costume, right?)

If horror movies, even the not so-scary ones, aren’t the vibe you’re looking for, let good ol’ TV help set the tone for All Hallows' Eve. From classic shows like The Brady Bunch to more modern favorites like Abbott Elementary, there is no shortage of great Halloween-themed episodes to binge before November 1. In the spirit of Halloween, Esquire’s entertainment editors spent some time digging through our favorites to settle on the best 30 Halloween episodes ever.

Grab some candy corn, put on your coziest jack-o'-lantern socks, and settle on the couch. These are the best Halloween-themed episodes of TV ever made.

Ted and John’s annual tradition of throwing eggs at bad Halloween costumes is thwarted when Blaire ropes Ted into being her designated driver for a party. Between Blaire’s impressive Catwoman costume, a killer gag referencing the Milgram Experiment, and a surprisingly moving story about seizing the day, Ted somehow has one of the best Halloween episodes in the entire streaming era.

One of the most popular episodes of Bob’s Burgers is this Halloween episode from season 6. When Louise claims she’s never been frightened by anything, the family tries to scare her good with their own haunted house. Just as the kids are ready to write off their parents’ lame efforts, the house seems to come alive. Stay for the credits to hear the hot new single from Boyz 4 Now, “I Love You So Much (It’s Scary).”

“Home” is one of the darkest episodes in all of The X-Files, which is really saying something. Originally aired in October 1996, the episode sees Scully and Mulder investigate a secluded farm home in Pennsylvania. While the episode is basically a tribute to American horror classics like The Texas Chain Saw Massacre, “Home” delivers its own hair-raising scares in the Peacocks, a family of violent inbreds. Though The X-Files was limited in what it could show onscreen due to broadcast restrictions, its suggestive nature lends the episode its pitch-black power.

For quintessential ‘90s sitcom antics, look no further than Home Improvement. The first Halloween episode of the series mostly centers on Brad being in his first-ever fight with his girlfriend, which only Tim can help him sort out. But the Halloween stuff happens in the subplot, where Tim and Al build a haunted house to scare the daylights out of one smug kid in particular.

Zoinks! This immaculate crossover from Supernatural’s thirteenth season feels ready-made for an All Hallows’ Eve marathon. (Never mind that it aired in March 2018.) In “Scoobynatural,” Sam, Dean, and Castiel get sucked into a television set and crash the Scooby-Doo, Where Are You? episode “A Night of Fright Is No Delight.” The Scooby Gang has met a lot of pop culture legends—Dick Van Dyke, Batman, John Cena—but their outing with the Supernatural guys marks the first time they ever tangled with genuine mystical evil.

Decades of Halloween parties are our way into Mr. Peanutbutter’s psyche and patterns of problematic behavior. In this season 5 episode of BoJack Horseman, the annoyingly upbeat Mr. Peanutbutter throws his annual Halloween party at BoJack’s place, which happens to be ground zero for his biggest relationships coming apart. Set against four different Halloween parties—in 1993, 2004, 2009, and 2018—audiences find new angles to a staple character we thought we had figured out.

South Park has been on the air long enough to have several Halloween episodes, but this season 1 hit is still the best. After Kenny is killed (again), he’s brought back to life as a zombie, whose condition is mistaken for a severe case of pinkeye. As “pinkeye” spreads through South Park, the boys try to celebrate Halloween with some very questionable costumes.

In Abbott Elementary, Halloween festivities descend into madness when a stolen bag of candy turns all the kids into hyperactive, sugar-infused monsters straight out of 28 Days Later. As the teachers try to corral the student body, Janine stresses over a Halloween party invitation. The episode caps off with a perfect Avengers: Infinity War reference.

This season 5 episode of Boy Meets World is technically not a Halloween special at all. It premiered in February 1998. But when teen slashers like Scream and I Know What You Did Last Summer were ruling the box office, Boy Meets World got in on the craze with a surprisingly emotional episode that interrogates Shawn’s guilt over the breakup of Cory and Topanga. During detention, the characters get locked inside the school with a masked killer on the loose.

After the Brady boys scare the girls with a fake ghost, the girls strike back with a taste of their own medicine. “Fright Night” is classic Brady Bunch antics, as the whole household gets in on the fun. Never mind that at this point in season 4, Greg and Marcia look old enough to be at parties instead of coming up with pranks.

It's Halloween at Cloud 9, which is an oddly fitting time for them to find a dead body in the store. A creepy employee, likely peeping in on the women's restroom, apparently got stuck and starved to death in the walls. With hours to spend before the coroner can get the body, everyone at Cloud 9, employees and shoppers alike, must go about their day as a rotten corpse watches over them all.

The Paddy's gang experiences a real scream when Dee gets pregnant, and everyone retraces their steps to figure out what the hell happened at their Halloween party. Leave it to It's Always Sunny in Philadelphia to turn their Halloween episode into a Rashomon scenario.

Before Deadpool, Ryan Reynolds was a sitcom star in the forgotten but outrageously funny Two Guys, a Girl, and a Pizza Place. In the show's first Halloween episode from season 2, it breaks all its rules by unleashing an evil doppelgänger of Reynolds' Berg who hacks his way through the group. Unfortunately, you cannot stream Two Guys, a Girl, and a Pizza Place on any platform in the U.S. But the heroes over at Shout! Studios produced a DVD box set that deserves a spot on your shelf.

When Ben starts living with Andy and April, he has to put up with their (unannounced) Halloween party. But while most of the episode follows conventional All Hallows hijinks, the real attraction is the cold open where Andy and April talk about “blood orphans” that sealed it as a classic. Would it surprise you to know that Widow’s Bay creator Katie Dippold wrote this one?

You can make the reasonable argument that It's the Great Pumpkin, Charlie Brown is still the greatest Halloween special ever made. It gave us Snoopy’s World War I battle with the Red Baron, Lucy and the football, “I got a rock,” and so much more. Honestly, without Great Pumpkin, we wouldn't be here making this list at all.

Just days before The Walking Dead premiered and became a cable TV monster, Community spoofed zombie movies with the hilarious and memorable “Epidemiology.” At the shockingly well-decorated Greendale Halloween party, some bad mystery meat turns the partygoers into grotesque husks with a taste for flesh. The episode peaks in the climax, when Troy reclaims his impressive Aliens loader cosplay to save his friends. He fails, but it’s still a cool moment.

All of Buffy the Vampire Slayer is tailor-made for Halloween. But in season 2, the cult series delivered a banger with the simply-titled episode, “Halloween.” When Buffy and the gang buy cursed costumes, they spend Halloween night believing they are their costumed alter egos—including Buffy, whose badass slayer skills are neutered when she believes she’s a dainty noblewoman from the 18th century.

If you think this Power Rangers Zeo episode has the mighty morphin’ heroes clashing with a pumpkin monster or something, think again. In this Halloween special from the 1996 iteration of the show, the Green Ranger, Adam (Johnny Yong Bosch), falls asleep while watching a horror movie marathon. He then wakes up in a black-and-white world of vampires, werewolves, and mummies. Here, the Power Rangers pay tribute to the Universal Monsters, plus a few karate kicks.

One year after Stranger Things became a Netflix juggernaut, it returned for its blockbuster second season. The premiere takes place during the greatest day of all: Halloween. While it is just the first chapter of another long and terrifying ordeal with the Upside Down, “Trick or Treat, Freak” is full of hijinks and house parties, and some of the sickest Ghostbusters costumes I’ve ever seen.

You know what's scarier than a ghost? Firing someone. In the first Halloween-themed episode of The Office, Michael Scott is forced by corporate to fire one of his employees before EOD. While later episodes of The Office would crank up the volume on the outrageousness—including a wildly controversial cold open from season 6 that was pulled from DVD and streaming—“Halloween” represents exactly the sort of banal black comedy that made the series so great in the first place.

From its perfect cold open to Jake proposing to Amy, “HalloVeen” takes the belt (sorry, cumberbund) as not just one of the best episodes of Brooklyn Nine-Nine but among the best Halloween episodes in any TV show. It’s the fifth annual Halloween Heist at the Ninety-Ninth Precinct, but this time, the stakes are higher than ever. Normally, the Halloween episodes of Brooklyn Nine-Nine are skippable filler, but given how this one cements Jake and Amy’s relationship in stone, fans can’t miss this one.

Friends might have some great Christmas and Thanksgiving episodes, but season 8's “The One with the Halloween Party” tops them all. As the pals throw a costume party in their improbably large New York City apartment, Phoebe runs into her twin sister, Ursula, and her fiancé (played by guest star Sean Penn). Peep some corporate synergy, as this Warner Bros.-produced sitcom dressed up Monica and Phoebe as Catwoman and Supergirl.

This show aired for just one season, so it has only one Halloween episode. But it’s a great one. Lindsay thinks she’s too cool to get into Halloween, but her mom wants her to stay home and pass out candy to trick-or-treaters. Meanwhile, we get some pretty solid costumes (“I’m not a girl, I’m a... Bionic Woman!”) from Sam, Bill, and Neal, who take to the streets for one last childhood All Hallows’ Eve.

Only Larry David would accuse trick-or-treaters of rigging the system. In this episode from season 2, Larry gets irritated after some older kids end up at his doorstep. But he gets what's coming to him when he wakes the next morning to find his backyard vandalized. “I didn't know there was going to be felony or treat!”

What starts as a pretty normal Halloween episode becomes a love letter to filmmaker Alfred Hitchcock. In this season 3 episode of That ‘70s Show, the guys feel stuck between childhood and adulthood while watching a Hitchcock movie marathon on Halloween. But when they suspect that Bob has murdered Midge, they spring into action, Rear Window style.

You can spend all of Halloween watching every Treehouse of Horror special from The Simpsons. There are dozens of them. Literally. But “Halloween of Horror” from season 27—the first time that The Simpsons ever did a “normal” Halloween episode—is a strong episode that uses the season to explore real emotions like fear and bravery. After a traumatic night at Krustyland, Lisa has a hard time getting over her new fear of everything.

It’s Halloween in New York City, and what’s Ted Mosby doing? Waiting for the Slutty Pumpkin. This season 1 episode of How I Met Your Mother playfully rips off Charlie Brown as Ted, dressed in a comically dated costume, waits for the mysterious girl who got away all those years ago at a Halloween party. Years later in season 7, Ted finally closed the book on the Slutty Pumpkin when she surfaced at last, and was played by guest star Katie Holmes.

In the first Halloween special for Modern Family, Claire tries to claim Halloween as her holiday (after Gloria had seized on Christmas and Thanksgiving) by creating the greatest haunted house in the neighborhood. While the episode is primarily concerned with Claire opening up about feeling sidelined in holiday traditions, this season 2 episode soars when Mitchell, dressed as Spider-Man, tries to avoid embarrassment at work... by climbing out of the building.

When the Marvel Cinematic Universe returned for Phase Four, it did so with WandaVision, the franchise's first Disney+ series. A tribute to TV sitcoms, “All-New Halloween Spooktacular!” grounded the show in the late ‘90s and early ‘00s with a Malcolm in the Middle-style episode where Uncle Pietro (guest star Evan Peters, whose appearance bridges the X-Men franchise to the MCU) comes to hang out on Halloween. Peep Elizabeth Olsen and Paul Bettany donning their characters' original costumes from the comics.

New Girl knew how to have fun on Halloween, and in season 2, it used the spooky season to interrogate the scariest thing in dating: situationships. When Jess takes a job working as a zombie at a haunted house, her not-that-serious boyfriend Sam comes to see her—but Nick finds out just how detached he is from her. Meanwhile, Schmidt and Cece wind up with an “accidental” couples costume.

#7009. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.

#7007. This Is the Right Way to Do a Gallery Wall, According to a Framing Expert

It’s the artful update your space has been waiting for.

Framed art never goes out of style, and gallery walls are one of the go-to ways to display cherished photos and prints. For Tara Gavin, the founder of On the Wall Framing in Toronto, the secret to doing a gallery wall right is to make sure it has a “design direction.”

“My team and I love playing with scale, unexpected mat colors, different frame finishes, and the way photographs are positioned within a frame in order to keep them fresh,” Gavin says.

Gavin’s approach proves that gallery walls can look unexpectedly artful instead of off-kilter. Here’s how she intentionally designs gallery walls to make them a standout feature, plus, her three rules to follow so that you can do the same.

The easiest solution for any gallery wall is to stick to white backdrops and wood frames. And while a classic approach will always look great, Gavin wanted to give the above space an edge. She kept the familiar oak and black frames for some pieces, and then distinguished others with burgundy-hued mats and frames.

“The burgundy introduces warmth and richness, while the oak keeps everything from feeling too formal,” she says. “The black frames provide a little contrast and help tie the composition together.”

You can follow suit with the same palette, or you can use this pop of color to coordinate with a prominent shade in the surrounding furnishings—the results will be eye-catching, while still allowing the gallery wall to blend in with the rest of the room. “We tend to think of white as the default, but a rich burgundy, soft blue, olive green, or even a warm neutral can completely transform the look of a photograph,” Gavin adds.

One of the most interesting aspects of Gavin’s gallery wall approach is the use of negative space: the size of the mat around the image. She notes that it’s one of her team’s favorite tools for making a photo feel special, since it inadvertently makes you want to lean in for a better look at the shot. And given that the smaller size of the images naturally creates more room, Gavin also recommends using this as an opportunity to stick more than one photo into a frame. Then she’d play around with a composition that feels right.

“Rather than always centering a single photo, we often use multiple openings within one frame or place photos slightly off-center,” she says. “It's a small detail, but it makes the whole gallery wall feel more interesting and a little less predictable.”

Although it isn’t always necessary, Gavin recommends printing images in black and white for a uniform look, as was the case with the above project.

“One of the biggest challenges with family photographs is that they're often taken at different times, in different lighting conditions, and with completely different color palettes,” she says. “Converting them to black and white can bring a sense of consistency to the collection.”

Since you’ll already be planning out the positioning of your gallery wall, devote the same consideration to the photos that will go in it. The black-and-white composition will make the pictures look cohesive as a display, but the memories they stir up will be much more dynamic.

“People sometimes forget that the photographs sitting on their phones or tucked away in boxes can become some of the most meaningful art in their homes,” Gavin says. “You don't need an expensive art collection to create a beautiful wall. Sometimes the most compelling gallery walls are built around the memories and moments that matter most to you.”

#7006. Watch 9-1-1 for the Volcano Eruptions and Bee-nadoes. Stay for the Heart.

The first responder drama understands grief and the power of found families better than any other series on TV.

But 9-1-1 is also a tough show to stay up to date with—there are over 140 episodes across 10 seasons, not including the spin-offs. Critics call it trashy but, really, it’s absurd excellence. Me and the 9 million average viewers of the most recent season can’t be wrong. 9-1-1 is epic, must watch television. So, after a brief time away, I returned to 9-1-1 because I heard that something crazy occurred, even by 9-1-1’s standards. And no, I’m not talking about the volcano eruption.

Midway through season 8, 9-1-1 killed off a major character. For seven years on TV, Peter Krause played the soul of the fire station, Captain Bobby Nash. Then, in season 8, episode 15, he contracts a deadly fever during a chemical lab outbreak and decides to quarantine himself inside. Before he dies, he hands the sole antidote to his appointed successor, Howard “Chimney” Han (Kenneth Choi). Major character deaths are not new to TV. Game of Thrones famously dispatched characters like the series was pulling petals off daisies while playing, “He loves me, he loves me not.” But after eight seasons spent with Krause’s character basically serving as the paternal guiding hand of the series, it was an emotional blow.

Around the same time, I received news that my grandfather had terminal late-stage cancer. Due to distance—I live in New York City, while he lives in China—I realized that I would be unable to travel to say goodbye at his side. It was devastating, and I didn’t know how I would manage roughly 7,000 miles away from my family. We watch TV to be entertained and be comforted from the stress of our daily lives. We also tend to create parasocial relationships with our favorite TV characters. So, following my grandfather’s diagnosis, mourning the loss of someone so central to your family like the fictional Captain Nash helped me to grieve.

I originally avoided the show the best I could once images leaked of the cast carrying a casket through the streets of Los Angeles. Usually, 9-1-1’s promotion is enough to draw you back in. You’ve most likely seen the viral ads for 9-1-1 containing bee-nadoes, tsunamis on Santa Monica, or giant inflatable runaway pumpkins. But despite the wacky premises, 9-1-1 was something that I was relegated to watching alone—mostly because my boyfriend would groan and exit the room every time I had it on our TV. Plus, when I bring it up amongst friends and family, I’m usually met with raised brows and derision. But I’m tired of pretending that 9-1-1 isn’t amazing TV. It isn’t solely the ridiculous emergencies that keeps me watching: It’s the genuine portrayal of the first responders and fire station’s found family.

Following Captain Nash’s death, the most recent season focused on the aftermath among those he left behind. The tremendous loss affected every character on the show, including the tough but vulnerable lead, police officer Athena Grant (Angela Bassett). As Grant, Bassett faces an array of life-or-death situations with compassion and ferocity every episode. Together with Krause’s Captain Nash, who played her husband, they faced a lot of fires and even one cruise ship invaded by pirates. They got through it by sticking together.

Aside from the leads, 9-1-1 also features the strong and smart Henrietta Wilson (Aisha Hinds). Jennifer Love Hewitt plays Maddie Buckley, a 9-1-1 operator and domestic abuse survivor. Her husband, Han, steps up as the new captain of the firehouse. But the character I find most compelling is Evan “Buck” Buckley (Oliver Stark), the impulsive, adrenaline chasing firefighter.

Buck is the beating heart of the show, starting out as a brash and immature member of the firehouse before growing into a more level-headed and responsible emergency responder. His close friendship with the single-father firefighter, Edmundo “Eddie” Diaz (Ryan Guzman), is constantly shipped by fans of the show in a fervor that would make Spock and Kirk stans blush. (The passion from fans rooting for the two to get together has only increased since Buck came out as bisexual in season seven.) But the show wants you to root for them, often spotlighting how the two characters would literally die in a blaze of glory just to save the other.

Throughout my years watching 9-1-1, these characters became something of my found family as well. Seeing every character struggle with the loss of Captain Nash in their own ways—like reconnecting with religion, numbing themselves with substance abuse, or facing their fears—helped me accept that my grief might manifest differently than I expected. It was cathartic to see all the ways that the pain would be survivable with the help of my loved ones.

The tenth season of 9-1-1, which premiered on ABC on October 15, teases an erupting volcano in the middle of Los Angeles. That’s just good TV, folks. But as the season carries on, the show spreads a message of optimism for humanity—proving every episode that moving with compassion and relying on community helps us build resiliency no matter what absurd tragedies we may face. I don’t care if the volcano takes Los Angeles back to the prehistoric era, I know that firehouse 118 will get through it with the power of their friendship and their boundless determination to do good. Amidst our constant news cycle dominated by bad actors, we could all do with a little dose of hope.

#7004. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

#7003. You Can Now Steal Jeremiah Brent’s Signature Style With His New Rug Collection on Amazon

The muted tones will work seamlessly in any room.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A rug can be one of a designer's most important, transformative tools: it can anchor furniture, establish a color palette, and make a room feel inherently cozier. If you doubt a rug’s superpowers, Jeremiah Brent might have you thinking otherwise. The celebrity designer just teamed up with Loloi on a collection of rugs that bring the focus underfoot.

While most rug collections are packed with stripy or floral-fueled layers, Brent found inspiration in architecture: If you look closely, you’ll spot rugs with tile-inspired repeats, “carved” silhouettes, and strong structural lines. Take the Obelisk collection, for example, where a plush, hand-knotted wool surface is expertly paired with notched corners. You know, the kind you might see in a historic home on a vintage wooden chair.

If you want to command more attention, check out the Fori Collection. The assortment of meticulously uniform patterns evokes the soothing repetition of mosaic tiles. Or, for something down the straight and narrow, the angular lines of the Axis Collection will look great in modern homes. Plus, the Axis’s 100 percent polyester construction is easy to clean, making it a great choice for high-trafficked areas like a hallway or playroom.

Not only are all three rug collections available on Amazon, so you can add them to your next Prime order, but they all come in muted nature tones. Slightly autumnal, yet versatile enough to look good year-round.

While Brent’s Loloi line is reasonably priced—it’s rare to find a five-foot by seven-foot option for under $250—adding a rug to your space can be both a big financial and spatial undertaking. If you’re not willing to move your furniture or buy a new layer, there are also decorative throw pillows for $100 a pop.

Regardless of which layers you add to your cart, one thing’s for sure: This drop is poised to beautify your home from the ground up.

#7001. The 8 Best High-End Coffee Makers of Every Variety

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you're looking for drip, espresso, or cold brew—this is how to get your caffein fix.

Every true coffee drinker knows that a bad cup can ruin an otherwise good morning. At home, good coffee is consistency, it’s comfort, it is often what gets you out of bed in the morning. If you get a coffee machine that starts to deliver substandard cups, you start to question your purchase... and your sanity.

It’s not just about a perfect cup of Joe; espresso drinkers deserve the luxury of an easy at-home solution that allows them to make the perfect latte or Americano, and repeat that process daily. It doesn’t matter who you are, you deserve not to stress about good coffee. Your order might not be simple, but these machines keep it easy breezy.

I've rounded up eight of my fellow Esquire editor's favorite high-end coffee machines (including a few of my personal picks) that go above-and-beyond. From elevated drip offerings to single-serve delights to automated espresso heaven— there’s even one machine entirely dedicated to cold brewing—I’ve included everything a coffee drinker could ask for. Built-in grinders? They have it. Milk foam wand? Of course. Or just want a plain old drip option that’s elevated? I’ve got you covered there too.

These are the cafe quality coffee machines that do all the work for you (or don’t, if that’s what you’re after) and look great doing it. With any one of them, you'll regularly be making top-notch coffee at home in no time.

For your first high-end coffee maker, there's no machine more universally loved than the Moccamaster. It's relatively small, counter-friendly, and made simply so that it lasts for decades. It also just looks better than anything else on the market.

Here's its brew deal: Functionally, it is like a drip machine where you set it and forget it. But in practice, it is actually an automated pour-over machine. There is a nine-hole outlet that drips water in stages, at strategically placed times and at specific temperatures, so that you get a proper bloom and extraction phase. It takes a pretty quick six minutes, but the drawback is you can only brew a full or half carafe—no single cup. But if you are looking for the best non-espresso coffee maker in the business, the list could pretty much start and end right here.

Once you get past (or if you can get past) the sticker shock, Balmuda's coffee machine is bringing something special to the easy-cup-of-coffee table. The small-capacity machine makes just about a cup and a half of coffee, but it is as good of a cup and a half of coffee as you'll ever find. There are a few controls—three sizes and regular, strong, and ice brew options—perhaps a few more than you would expect or want.

However, the brew process is kind of extraordinary to watch. The water comes out in small, steamy drips at specific intervals and temperatures to get the most out of the grounds. The execution is just about perfect, though I find the water doesn't hit the grounds equally. The middle can end up over-extracted and the sides under. I find it brews better when I push some of the grounds toward the center so they get equally saturated, but for a coffee machine that costs $700, I would prefer the water head was a little bigger so that it would do this job for me.

“High-end” can mean different things to different people. If you want to move on from a Nespresso pod machine, this is would be our first port of call in figuring out if you will enjoy the life of “espresso guy.” Don’t go onto the expensive stuff until you have at least tried one of these.

What sets the Breville Bambino apart from other value-focused espresso machines is that it has the chops to be an end-game machine. On the build side, that is because the body of the machine is stainless steel, and the accents have the weight you get in the higher-end machines. On the brew side, it’s because it does a true low-pressure pre-infusion—a crucial part of the espresso process—and has incredible temperature control.

The main thing we think really sets it up as an in-between machine is the portafilter. It’s a 54-mm basket, the standard commercial size, and it has the option to go non-pressurized like commercial machines, or pressurized like more budget machines. What does that mean? If you have a good espresso-level grinder—since that is often a more important step—this machine can be as good as the eye-wateringly expensive ones you’re about to see. But, if you aren’t at that level, a pressurized basket can pull a decent shot with even Café Bustelo (no hate, we still drink it).

If you don’t need another difficult learning curve in your life (trust me, I relate), you might want something a tad easier than the De’Longhi. You don’t need to go fully automatic to turn espresso brewing into an intuitive three-step process. According to coffee snobs, the grind selection and tamping processes are the important and fun part. This machine keeps that intact, while boiling it down (hehe) to just selecting your grind size, dose, and then you can take the mathematically correct number of grounds for your drink of choice and tamper it and then let the machine pull your perfect shot.

It unfortunately doesn’t have some of the more handy automatic cleaning functions of the more full-auto machines. This means weekly upkeep, especially if you make frequent use of the milk wand, which for some people is an absolute no go.

This is the espresso machine to get if you’re dedicated to learning. It will make your coffee and be your teacher all in one. First off, the thing just looks great. I’m a big fan of the cherry red and walnut color scheme myself. Inside, it’s basically a big computer dedicated to brewing the best espresso possible, and to make sure you improve it will even give suggestions over time—pro tip, if something’s off it’s usually your grind size. You still get all the fun of precision espresso making, with a wand for you to steam your own milk to boot, only you will actually learn how to pull a better shot; you won’t just stumble into it or, conversely, have a machine do your thinking for you.

Now, this is the Series 1 of a technology-based product, which means it may come with its fair share of potential issues. You may get one that works flawlessly. Or... a random system error can cause the whole thing to brick or the screen to be completely unusable. If it breaks, Fellow customer-service is genuinely interested in helping you out, so don’t be afraid to be a first-gen adopter. That said, I’m hoping a Series 2 will roll out a touch more seamlessly.

This is without a doubt the most high-end espresso machine on the market right now. The rotary pump, dual boiler, and three-tier setup combine to make for a foolproof semi-auto machine that still leaves room for experimenting. Seriously, the shots we’ve been pulling from this have been consistent to an almost scary degree. Through all the bean switches, recipe tweaking, and shot dialing, the coffee was always passable. The dedicated tier for the grinder keeps your beans away from the hot water. In most coffee makers, they sit on top and over time this exposure to heat can lead to burnt beans and worse coffee.

What you end up with is the difference between needing to dial in the right settings, and the freedom to experiment. The Meraki machine is always the latter, giving you room to grow your hobby without making bad coffee along the way.

DiFor as much as I love my KitchenAid automatic coffeemaker, the Magnifica Duo is a much better value. It’s one of the rare consumer machines where you can sit back and genuinely let it do all the work. It gives more options too, which is exactly what people want from an automatic espresso machine. Absolutely no fuss and the ability to choose any drink from the screen on a whim. It’s as easy as selecting what you want and waiting a couple of minutes. That’s the magic of fully automatic (or superautomatic) espresso.

If you’re really into the milk and foam art side of being a barista, this is the high-end machine to get. The dual milk frothing tank can make hot or cold foam from any kind of milk or milk alternative. From the touch-screen menu, you can adjust foam texture and tell it which dairy alternative you’re using too. But, really, at the end of the day you can still just put in whatever you have and tell it to make you a latte. The result will be tasty enough. If you care more than that, you can start making adjustments; just don’t try to dive too deep, it is fully automatic for a reason.

Remember when I said that if you wanted cold brew, you should get a dedicated machine for that? Well, here we are. This is the Cumulus and it’s overwhelmingly the easiest and quickest option for at home cold brew there is. It has three options that let you brew cold brew, nitro, and cold espresso. After just a 15 minute warm up (or cool down, I supposed) you’ll be able to brew any of the three options in just five minutes.

There is, however, a catch. The Cumulus makes really good cold brew because it brews from pods filled with coffee concentrate; not grounds. To do this you need to buy Cumulus’s proprietary pods because, right now, it is the only one in the game. That means you’re really at the whims of the company in terms of pricing. And if the company ever goes under, despite you loving your Cumulus, you won’t be able to keep using it without access to more pods.

Manual or Lever Machines (none featured): We could add a lever machine to this list. But, having tried and owned models, they are simply too fussy for the purposes of this story. If you’re on that level, we assume you don’t need this particular list.

Semi-automatic Espresso Machines: This is the bulk of the category. Everything from the La Marzocco your barista uses, the Lelit your boss raves about, and the entry-level Breville we mention. You are responsible for the entire process, so there is plenty of room to mess up, but they are not “manual” because of the existence of truly manual lever machines. If you have dreams about getting “serious” about your at-home barista hobby, this is the category you want to aim at.

Super Automatic Espresso Machines: This is the category where everything is done for you. Typically, you interact with a touch screen. You fill up the milk and water tanks, press cortado, and your drink is made for you. That’s it. You get to choose your beans and save money on expensive cafe drinks, but you’re not really tasked with much work. Get this if you don’t want to faff about with becoming a coffee guy.

What to Look for in High-End Coffee Makers

The size on some coffee machines can feel way different in person from how they appear online, particularly if you need to fill a reservoir or a burr grinder. You might need clearance, not just for the machine but for around the machine. When you're sizing your space, take into account the space on any side of the machine.

Size can be, but isn't necessarily, indicative of capacity. And when you get into these pricier high-end coffee machines, you'll notice capacity generally goes down. That's because the focus is on making a single great cup of coffee, often with methods that are more involved than a drip. When we are talking drip, we mean brew capacity, aka how much it can make at once. When talking espresso, it’s all about tank capacity; how much water can it hold at once before you need to refill?

For some people, automation is the entire point of getting a high-end coffee maker, as opposed to what we would call an espresso machine that's manual. And for that, we understand. Most of these are semi-automated, but we tried to include something for everyone, including one superautomatic machine.

#7000. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

#6998. 5 MAJOR Mistakes You're Making in Your Living Room Design, According to Designers

A living room has to accommodate a lot of life. Truly a multipurpose space, it needs to be a quiet theater for TV, a bespoke den for hosting, and a cozy place to lounge or read. With all the roles that the space has to satisfy, it’s no wonder homeowners struggle to find the perfect way to decorate.

“A beautiful living room that’s uncomfortable to sit in, difficult to navigate, or poorly lit will never truly feel stylish, no matter how visually appealing it may be,” says designer Mindy Kelson O’Connor. “No matter how beautiful a room is, if people have to do gymnastics to make it work, absolutely no one will focus on the pretty elements.”

Designers are often called in when style and function become too incongruent to ignore, so you can bet that they’ve seen their share of tragic, tacky, and disorganized living rooms. “A room that’s not functional is quite literally useless,” says Rachel Sherman, principal at Rachel Sloane Interiors. “Think about rooms that you love hanging out in, then consider why. It’s probably because they're easy and comfortable to be in, while also feeling beautiful.”

If that vision doesn’t match your current reality, read on as four designers share the biggest mistakes you could be making in your living room—and how to fix them. Put this advice into practice, and your living room may actually do it all.

In the rush to get settled into your home, there’s a good chance that the layout you landed on when you first moved in is still the layout you’re working with now. But if the furniture resembles an obstacle course or creates the feeling of too much airiness, it’s time to reconsider where everything is positioned.

“Furniture that doesn't encourage conversation, or doesn't consider flow, can feel uninviting. A good layout supports both socializing and solitude,” says Sarah Magness, the principal and founder of Studio Magness. The layout should be as intuitive as possible, and there should always be a surface near a seat where you can place a drink.

Do you entertain often? Sherman would make sure you have seating solutions for every guest, whether that means stashing two ottomans between the coffee table and fireplace (she recommends moveable pieces!) or upgrading a loveseat to a sectional. Don’t get too caught up in how things are “supposed to” look; find a solution based on how people will move through the room.

“It’s easy to become overly focused on symmetry or anchoring everything around a central fireplace or architectural element,” O’Connor says. “But a truly functional living room prioritizes conversation and ease. Designing solely for a visual focal point often means sacrificing seating arrangements that foster relaxed socializing.”

While overhead lighting makes it easy to transition from day to night, it doesn’t do much to add ambiance. “This makes a living room feel like a conference room instead of a warm gathering in someone’s home,” Sherman says. If you’d rather feel warm and cozy—and this is the place for that, after all—then invest in alternative sources.

“Layered lighting is so important, especially in a living room, because it allows for flexibility and creates mood,” O’Connor says. “A central overhead decorative fixture or two, depending on the space’s length, is great for lively parties or game nights. It can also create visual interest and style.” Add a floor lamp beside a side chair for reading, a table lamp near a sectional, and a pair of sconces flanking a fireplace, and you’re all set.

No one wants to worry about stains on their sofa when friends are over to watch the big game, and yet, it’s common to secretly wish for those squeaky plastic sofa covers of the past whenever there’s a party. If you’re someone who wants to guarantee that furniture stays pristine while hosting regularly, then consider investing in finishes that let you exhale.

“Using precious or high-maintenance fabrics discourages everyday use,” Magness says. “Fortunately, many high-performance fabrics are now designed to handle everyday wear and spills, so there's no need to sacrifice durability for style.”

Speaking of creating harmony between form and function, be honest about how comfortable your sofa truly is. If it feels too rigid, too deep, or too shallow, then it might be time for a change. “An elegant sofa loses its charm if guests are constantly adjusting or grimacing while seated,” O’Connor adds.

The textiles of a living room can really make or break the space, particularly when it comes to the size of the rug. It’s not unheard of to have one that’s too small, making it so the coffee table floats on top while the rest of the furniture awkwardly surrounds it. “This unintentionally makes the room feel smaller,” Sherman says.

The replacement rug should be large enough to sit at the center of the room, with the main furnishings all touching it. “Items against the wall don’t need the rug underneath as well, but the center of the room should have abundant coverage,” Sherman adds. A well-sized rug will make the room feel more complete, alongside pillows for softness and layering. If you do fall in love with an impractical fabric, this is your opportunity to go for it. “Use highly decorative or expensive fabrics for drapery or decorative pillows,” Magness says. Again, it’s all about finding a balance.

Interior designer Louis Lin wants you to stop putting your sofa’s back against the wall, literally. Instead of focusing on maximizing the amount of floor space, Lin suggests shifting your attention to the room’s “spatial dialogue.”

“The goal isn’t to maximize emptiness, but rather to choreograph the spatial flow and create visual rhythm,’ says Lin. “Always float a sofa off the wall, even by a few inches to reduce visual clutter.”

Intentionally placing your furniture not only leads to a more curated feel, but also allows you to create pathways for physical and visual flow, minimizing overcrowding and maximizing ease. After all, you shouldn’t be dodging corners in your own home.

#6996. Finally, an Air Fryer That Isn't Embarrassing to Own

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Ninja Crispi works just as good as it looks.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

I’ve been anti-air fryer for a half-decade now. Not anti for any functional reason; in that regard, the entire category is perfect top to bottom. I used to have one I got at Walmart for $30, and, functionally, it’s as good as whatever the air fryer du jour might be. Reheated leftovers came out like brand new, chicken wings were restaurant-crisp, and it took all of 30 seconds to clean. It was big and ugly and plastic, but there was enough cabinet space to put it away, so that didn’t matter.

The problem came when my wife and I moved to New York about half a decade ago. Faced with a choice between a coffee machine and an air fryer—both bulky and specific—we chose the coffee machine. The air fryer got put on the sidewalk and had a new family within the day. Since then, we’ve learned to live like our ancestors—no air fryer, just roughing it with an old-school oven.

The crucial change came with a Ninja Crispi. We’re on a glass storage container kick, and the Ninja Crispi promised double duty: air frying and easy food storage. We made the jump. After one week of meal prepping with air-fried salmon, potatoes, and broccoli, we asked each other: How the fuck did we ever survive with the old air fryer?

The thing about air fryers is that the technology—which was invented in its modern iteration in 2010 for Philips—was essentially perfected when it was invented. Like my $30 version, any old souped-up convection oven that keeps food on a perforated cooking platform can cook crispy food. It’s genius in its simplicity.

So, I can’t exactly commend Ninja for making an air fryer that works—anyone could do that. I just want to tell you, reader, that it does. And though it takes a few tries to get the hang of the attachment system for the glass containers, it’s really just a simple slide and lock. You wear oven mitts to handle the container, but that’s the only thing that differs functionally. The rest is as simple as setting a temperature and time.

Now that I own it, this feels as simple as the act of air frying itself, but it’s a real design feat. Ninja uses shockproof and heat-tolerant borosilicate glass containers. I assume the problem that Ninja was initially going after was PFAS and PTFE, forever chemicals. When I was using my plastic air fryer in college, forever chemicals were just part of the cocktail of shit I was ingesting, but now I wouldn’t live like that. That’s the first problem solved.

The secondary effect of the glass container is that it solves the storage problem. I most often use an air fryer to meal prep. On Sunday and Wednesday nights, I’m setting some combination of protein, potatoes, and vegetable—enough for both my wife and myself to eat over the next few days. The old air fryer made that just annoying enough between cooking and storing. This one does it all. What I don’t pack on day one stays in the container, cools, gets a lid (included with the machine), and goes into the fridge. It’s simple usability, but it makes my life infinitely easier.

All that said, my biggest issue with the air fryer market, truthfully, is my own vanity. I spend an inordinate amount of time thinking about the things that make my life beautiful: clothes and furniture; it’s why I work this job. My kitchen is an accessory to that. This is going to sound asinine, but when my wife and I have people over for dinner and are serving food in Le Creuset bakeware, heirloom Lodge cast iron, and end-grain wood boards—we don’t want them peeking in the kitchen and seeing that ugly old air fryer.

But that's just it. Nothing about a Ninja Crispi changes the way you use an air fryer. It’s just everything you never thought about, made to function better.

#6994. I’ve Tested This Suitcase on Three Continents, and It’s Still My Go-To for Every Trip

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

My long weekend on Maine’s coast, five-day escape to South America, and two-week trek across South Asia have one thing in common: the same suitcase set. I’ve had my Monos carry-on bag and check-in bag for over two years. They’ve followed me on domestic and long-haul international flights. And there’s a reason I reach for them before any other pieces. They’re the most durable luggage I’ve ever owned.

I can always rely on my Monos set for easy handling on the most complicated trips. It’s been tossed around by security professionals, rolled over rough terrain, and stuffed to the brim with souvenirs. When a product has been stress-tested like that, it’s earned my lifelong seal of approval. After two years of testing, here's everything I love about Monos luggage and why I'll still be using it for years to come.

Shop the Monos Classic Luggage Collection

The Monos Carry-On measures 22" x 14" x 9", and it has enough room for a five-day trip. It has the best maneuverability of any carry-on suitcase that I've tested. I've wheeled it on rough gravel roads, through puddles, and on airport carpet with zero issues. This suitcase is my go-to whenever I travel to small towns with less pavement and more cobblestone.

The compression panel design in the carry-on is genius—it opens toward the top of the suitcase rather than to the side. This means you can pack both sides of the suitcase at the same time, rather than one covering the other. The size is more conservative, fitting the typical U.S. airline restrictions, but it leaves room for plenty of my shoes and packing cubes. I never have to worry if I’m in the last boarding group. The carry-on always fits overhead. Plus, since it's such a sturdy build, I can put my heaviest items in the carry-on and avoid checked bag weight restrictions.

The Monos Check-In Medium measures 26.5" x 18.5" x 10.5", and its capacity is designed for a seven- to 21-day trip. (It also comes in a large version, but I chose the medium because I didn't want a suitcase that was too large for me to maneuver.) I used to be too nervous to travel with a suitcase that doesn’t expand. However, the Monos’ capacity has pleasantly surprised me at every turn. It always holds more than you think it can, and the outer shell is actually pretty forgiving. Just by using some well-placed packing cubes, I’ve packed two weeks of outfits for traveling the Malaysian Peninsula.

The zippers are so durable that I never worry about them tearing when it’s packed to the brim. It comes with organization, but it’s unfussy. The compartments and mesh dividers are made of a thin but durable canvas material, so they leave you with more room to pile clothes high. Compared to bulky compression panels that are tricky to work with, this is a much easier packing process.

The Monos suitcases are made of an aerospace-grade polycarbonate shell. This makes them extra durable and extra waterproof, which I learned the hard way. While waiting at an outdoor train station, I got stuck in a downpour with my two Monos suitcases. When my train finally arrived, I was so relieved that all of my items were still bone-dry. On a summer trip from Singapore to Thailand, my cousin’s suitcase cracked from the handling. My Monos had a cosmetic dent, but otherwise was unharmed. Each suitcase even undergoes rigorous factory testing, from drop tests to wheeling it on stairs and rough roads.

After over two years of use on both domestic and international travel, my Monos suitcases still roll like they’re brand new. No chips in the paint, and no scuffs you couldn’t buff out. My check-in finally sustained a dent this summer after two years of spotless travel. But even with my overpacking, there’s no damage to the zippers or the mesh compartments. I can’t say the same for previous suitcases. I’m confident these will remain my go-tos for years to come. I’ve had my Monos set for so long that my terrazzo pattern has been sadly discontinued, but they come in so many different colors that you will find one that suits your taste and travel style.

Monos luggage is absolutely worth buying, especially if you prioritize durability and easy maneuvering. They're long-lasting, stylish, and have TONS of room. My Monos suitcases made a believer out of a skeptic. I didn’t think I’d ever willingly buy a suitcase that didn’t expand, let alone enjoy using it. Yet I've never felt truly limited by its size, and it's made even the roughest travel routes feel like a breeze. It holds up to anything you can throw at it, and the slim profile fits in any overstuffed car trunk or tiny overhead compartment. It’s at a higher price point, but I’ve gotten luxury luggage before that was nowhere near Monos quality. It’s an investment you can count on.

#6993. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6991. The 14 Best Leather Colognes That Are Perfect for the Cold Weather Ahead

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Just as versatile as your favorite leather jacket.

You pass a shoe store, a real one with old-school styles, and catch that scent of new leather. You slip on an old beat up leather jacket and get that deep, squeaky earthy hit. You slide into a leather banquette at your town’s most popular restaurant. Ever wanted that scent, in any variety, bottled up? That’s what a leather fragrance gives you. Warm, rich, and earthy, and often with a smoky edge, they’re especially great for fall and winter, though the right one can work year-round.

Even vegetarians like myself can get behind a sexy leather cologne; they’re not distilling real animal leather, if you were wondering. Since leather has no extractable essential oil that can be used for perfume, perfumers recreate its scent using synthetic or botanical accords, often blended with smoky, resinous notes like birch tar, cade, and labdanum. It’s creating the idea of leather. The result can range from rugged and intense to soft and smooth like suede. There are plenty of different takes on leather fragrance out there, but these are our favorites.

This fragrance from Cremo mostly features white moss and amber, but the base notes of smooth suede come through at the end to create a warm, elevated aroma. Don’t be fooled by the low price point—you’ll still get a sophisticated scent of suede, though it’s less aggressive than its spicier and woodier alternatives.

This Vintage cologne from John Varvatos is about as manly as it gets. Its warm, woody overall scent with hints of patchouli and tobacco leaf underneath leave you with a long-lasting air of sophistication and refinement. The warm spice notes perfectly complement crisp autumn air while the patchouli and suede offer warmth during the winter like a fireplace in a cozy cabin.

There’s no mistaking the leather in this cologne—it permeates the entire package—but instead of being aggressive, it comes across as sophisticated. It’s a gentlemanly scent that’s surprisingly wearable for both day and night, thanks to the amber and patchouli at the base that give it a warm, herbal vibe. Think of it as less “cowboy” and more “oil baron.”

If Ombré Leather is Tom Ford’s easy, toned-down version of leather, Tuscan Leather is the aggressive, literal version. The leather almost overpowers everything else—almost—but the sweeter top notes allow for a delicious opening that adds smoothness to the leather and the warm, woody dry down lends a layered elegance. The leather here is top quality. Not new car smell, but perfectly worn-in bespoke shoes or the seats of a vintage Ferrari.

At first spray, the fruity plum and peach top notes take over, but pretty soon they give way to a deeper, more layered fragrance that really does call to mind the stacks of an old library. The sweetness doesn’t entirely go away, but as the florals and leather appear, like pages turning in a book, more depth is revealed.

Designed as an enhancer—a lighter fragrance to layer with others to change the vibe—this interpretation of leather smells almost exactly like the real thing. It’s slick, smooth, and warm, with a sharp edge that’s masculine in a ’70s Camaro kind of way. Sure, you can wear it as it was meant (that is, with another cologne), but we say try it by itself to really drive that leather home.

Tru Western’s Leather Vintage Label cologne collection is a great place to start when it comes to leather fragrances. Each of the five scents highlights a different aspect of leather, but No. 1 brings out woody notes—and a bit of fresh clementine—to give the leather accord a classic, straightforward backdrop that lets the leather accord be the star player. For something a bit richer with amber notes, try No. 4 (Deep Leather).

We’re big fans of Ranger Station, the Nashville-based niche fragrance brand known for its nature-inspired scents—and it all started with the Leather + Pine candle. After the candle took off, Ranger Station of course had to turn the scent into a wearable fragrance. Slightly different from the candle’s scent, the EDP is a blend of tanned leather, pine sap, amber, and sandalwood, which creates an alluring blend of sweet and savory.

We can’t talk about leather colognes without mentioning Malin + Goetz’s famous take on the scent. Simply called “leather,” it’s a rugged blend of florals, spices, and woods that emphasizes pepper and lily of the valley along with leather accord. And while it features a handful of floral notes, it never leans into powdery territory; it remains a solidly masculine scent, though it suits pretty much everyone.

For a bit of a more intense, spicy take on leather, Desert Nights is the cologne to go with. It’s inspired by Marrakech after sunset, layering luxurious oud and sandalwood with earthy patchouli and moss, and the spices up top carry through. It’s seductive and perfect for a date night or winter outing.

Arquiste is known for complex fragrances inspired by historical events and specific landscapes, and Nocturnality is one of our favorites—we even included it in our 2025 Cologne Awards. The inspiration for this one comes from mid-’90s New York City nightlife; picture an underground club with humid air, booze, cigarette smoke, and lots of people dancing. In a less abstract sense, that means latex, leather, and oud, which combine for a dark, sexy scent.

Most leather colognes lean on warmth, sweetness, and spice to complement the leather notes (which is partly why you often see a shift toward leather colognes in the winter), but this one does things a bit differently. Yes, there’s spice in the opening, but there’s also citrus that moves on to florals in the middle before drying down to earthy, musky notes. The leather is apparent all the way through, but it feels lighter and fresher than many others on this list.

There’s something very nostalgic about this fragrance but it doesn’t smell old-fashioned. Instead, it calls to mind dark rooms, sexy rendezvous, sweaty nights, and skin tight jeans. It’s smoky and spicy, but layered with musk and pungent black leather for an über masculine vibe that’s impossible to ignore. When you wear it, expect to be noticed—and for the compliments to come nonstop.

Perfumehead’s scents, like all the best fragrances, tell stories and paint pictures. This boozy, smoky juice is inspired by the cowboy-filled honky-tonks of the 1970s Sunset Strip, and one whiff is more than enough to take you there. The final effect of the fragrance isn’t especially Western or aggressive, but more cozy and sensual, like finding a vintage leather jacket that miraculously fits you like a glove.

#6988. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT.
