#6985. Tim McGraw Knows How to Make a Marriage Work

“There are times you don’t get along ... but that’s not a reason to walk out the door,” the country singer says in a new episode of “What I’ve Learned.”

Tim McGraw wants to set the record straight. He’s heard the rumors that he performed at the fabled Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce wedding this summer. But the three-time Grammy Award winner and recent inductee to the Country Music Hall of Fame reveals now that the headlines got it wrong.

“I didn’t perform at her wedding,” McGraw tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian in a new episode of “What I’ve Learned,” which you can listen to here. “That was a rumor,” he says.

But the “Live Like You Were Dying” singer confirms that he did attend the wedding with his wife, fellow country music powerhouse Faith Hill, as they celebrate 30 years of marriage together. He wishes the same for Swift. “Taylor’s always been close to our family, our girls,” McGraw says. “We couldn’t be happier for her to find Travis, who’s a cool guy as well.”

The lore between Tim McGraw and Taylor Swift goes back to the start of her own music career, with her 2006 single “Tim McGraw.” Then, in 2013, the two recorded McGraw’s “Highway Don’t Care” for his album Taste of Country. “She’s a special person, and one of the most talented people I’ve ever met,” McGraw says in the episode. “She’s the biggest artist in the world ... but the biggest thing that maybe gets overlooked sometimes with Taylor is the way she writes to connect with her audience. [It] is such an incredible thing.”

But did they leave the newlyweds any advice? What’s the secret to making a marriage work for three decades?

“Just a commitment that you want it to work,” McGraw tells Esquire. “People are so easy to walk away from troubled times or bad times, or times where you don’t get along. But going into a marriage and being in a marriage for a long time, you start to realize that, yeah, there are gonna be times you don’t like each other. There are times you don’t get along ... but that’s not a reason to walk out the door.”

According to McGraw, “We made a commitment early on in our lives that [leaving] wasn’t going to be our first response to any kind of fight or any kind of disagreement. Because the more you do that, that starts opening the door for that choice. It lessens the commitment, and it lessens the pride and respect that you have in your marriage.”

The singer adds that people change over time, which should not be a deal-breaker for any marriage. “You change as you grow older. And you change individually as you grow older, and you change as a couple as you grow older in time spent together. And so the beauty, I think, in a long marriage, is you get the chance to fall in love with a new person every so often. Because people change.”

The rest of McGraw’s “What I’ve Learned” conversation covers his growth as a father (which required him to learn patience, “another level of love that you didn’t know existed”), adapting to an empty-nest household, and the story of how he learned he would be inducted into the Country Music Hall of Fame.

He also offers some advice to today’s younger men. “Just get out, be social,” McGraw says. “Being a man is how you treat people and certainly how you treat women.” His 30 years of marriage can attest to that.

#6983. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

Increasingly, cyber criminals are sharing AI prompts, jailbreak techniques, and access to existing models that can be incorporated into fraud and phishing operations, according the latest AI Threat Report from Flashpoint.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

The dark granite countertops in my rental made my space feel so, well, dark.

I knew the process would likely involve plenty of trial and error, so I ordered two rolls from Amazon (you can buy the exact ones I used below). These come in a range of sizes, from 11.8” x 78.7” to 47.2” x 196”—pull out a measuring tape and choose the best option for your space.

I’ll be honest: The project was anything but easy, though that was partly because I tackled it solo. Yes, having a second set of hands would have made the process much smoother, as peeling off the backing and applying the contact paper can get tricky.

I’d also suggest buying this smoothing tool kit from Amazon, as it was a real game-changer for me. When you’re ready to get started, peel back a small section of the backing and carefully press the adhesive side onto the counter. I worked little by little, using the smoothing tool to eliminate any air bubbles as I went. There are also grid lines on the backing, making it easy to measure and trim the paper to fit your countertops.

My countertops before I installed the contact paper.

From start to finish, the project took me roughly seven hours on a Saturday. Again, part of that was because I was working alone, not to mention it was my first time using contact paper—and those pesky air bubbles were my biggest challenge. My countertops also have several curved edges, which only made the process harder.

Mine are far from perfect, but I don’t regret installing them in the slightest. Not only have they brightened up my kitchen tenfold, but they also haven’t budged in the year or so I’ve had them. If marble doesn’t align with your style, there are a slew of other iterations on Amazon, from wood finishes to stark white. The beauty of this contact paper lies in its versatility—it can also double as wallpaper or be used to spruce up a nightstand or dresser. I’ll leave the decision up to you.

#6979. Unraveling All the Details Behind Wicker’s Wicker

We asked the artists behind Alexander Skarsgård’s woven-to-order suit how they built it—and kept his appeal intact.

“It will take her another lifetime to understand wicker,” Marella Agnelli reportedly said of fellow socialite Mercedes Bass’s apartment. With Alexander Skarsgård’s help, some of us are getting there a little faster.

In Wicker, the romantic comedy based on Ursula Wills’s short story “The Wicker Husband,” Olivia Colman plays a fisherwoman who commissions a husband from the local basketmaker (Peter Dinklage). Skarsgård plays her woven-to-order spouse, whose resemblance to patio furniture does surprisingly little to diminish his sex appeal. Directed by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer, the film comes out in theaters today.

Behind the wicker transformation is Wētā Workshop, the New Zealand design and effects company whose credits include The Lord of the Rings and Avatar. Its artists make the costumes, props, and creatures that give imaginary worlds something tangible—and, in this case, marriageable.

For prosthetic makeup designer Joe Dunckley and lead makeup effects artist Sarah Rubano, the assignment was to turn Skarsgård into a convincing wicker husband without losing the expressions that make him recognizable. The team spent 18 months developing and manufacturing his suit and facial prosthetics, translating his features into a weave, right down to his furrowed brows.

Naturally, we had questions. How do you make wicker sexy? What happens to the eyelashes at lunch? And could anyone involved look at a household basket the same way again? Below, Dunckley and Rubano explain the work behind the wicker.

Before we get to the cheekbones, how did this assignment arrive?

Dunckley: We were approached by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer about 18 months out from filming. Initially, they were after a piece of concept art—a beautiful piece that they could take to potential cast members. This was part of their development process, so they had a tool for helping show their vision for the project.

Pretty quickly after that came the question: How are you going to make this wicker husband character? So much of the film’s success hinges on the success of that character. They always wanted to make the wicker husband a physical prosthetic character. That led us into a process of research and development. It was an 18-month period of R&D and manufacture to get to the final product.

A husband made to order. What, exactly, was in the order?

Dunckley: About 95% of the prosthetic and fabricated suit is made out of a 3D-printed material. Most prosthetic materials are manufactured to imitate human skin, but the wicker husband is built of wicker, not flesh and bones, so we knew we needed something different.

Sometimes we found the limits of existing programs and had to write our own code for weaving. Weaving is such complex geometry that it was very challenging for sculptors to weave in 3D.

The prosthetic appliances were then printed directly, without needing to go through the mold-making process.

Rubano: On [Skarsgård’s] body, we had about 50 individual pieces stitched and anchored onto thin spandex or mesh. The face, including the wig, was about 10 individual pieces. We also used foam latex for the lips, eye bags, and underlip and chin piece.

Did anyone get to weave an actual basket, or was this strictly a digital operation?

Dunckley: Absolutely. We had a team of weavers who had just come off Avatar, where they’d been weaving costumes for the Na’vi. Our design team would block out the shapes that we liked, and our lead weaver would do a physical weave sample using flax, giving us those shapes.

We’d print, do a makeup test, and learn what we learned: This one’s too loose. This one’s too tight and it binds. We’ll need more movement here. Slowly, we developed what you ended up seeing in the film.

I had a crush on the wicker husband. How do you make wicker handsome?

Dunckley: One of our main objectives was to make sure Alexander shines through the makeup. He’s a handsome man, and he has an expressive quality that’s really unique and recognizable.

We wanted to get those qualities into the weave. It was a matter of deconstructing what it is about those expressions that are endearing, that are heart-melting, and then reconstructing them in a weave pattern and enabling that movement for him through the prosthetic.

How long did it take for Alexander Skarsgård to become the wicker husband every day of filming?

Rubano: We got the whole morning process down to an hour and a half. That was the brief at the beginning, because he was working every day. It was an ambitious shooting schedule, so all the build techniques supported being able to expedite it.

He would come in, and we would put on a partial fabricated undersuit. He would come to the makeup chair, then go back and get more of the body suit on. Then we got into gluing the appliances to his face, the eyelashes, the wig, the styling, the makeup around the eyes—all the finishing touches.

And at lunch? Surely even a wicker husband gets a break.

Rubano: About halfway through, once we were really confident with our processes, we started giving him a bit of relief at lunchtime. I would take off the eyelashes because they’re quite robust, and he’s not used to wearing fake eyelashes. We would wrap up his beard and put a little bib over it so he could eat and be comfortable. We’d take off the gloves—just all the things we could do to give him some reprieve. After lunch, we’d build him up 100% again. The cleanup at the end of the day was about half an hour. The real challenge was keeping him cool. He runs hot, so we had fans and ice packs.

How do you feel about all the online chatter about “sexy wicker”?

Dunckley: We never would have expected this kind of level, to be honest. It’s been quite a ride to watch the enthusiasm, all the excitement, all the memes. We sat around watching memes for hours and laughing our heads off.

Rubano: We’ve built so many different types of characters for so many different types of films over 30-plus years. Sometimes they strike a chord as hoped, sometimes they don’t. Sometimes the chord has been struck louder than you could ever have expected. This is an example of that.

Has this changed how you look at the wicker in your own home?

Rubano: I have this tiny little wicker basket, and it’s not necessarily sexy, but I do love it. I look at it with love and adoration. It’s just this tiny little basket I put all my jewelry bits and pieces in. But, of course, the hottest basket of all is Alexander Skarsgård. Definitely.

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6978. A Jewel-Toned Chicago Home Inspired by Pakistani Culture

Kate Haynes of Noble Studio brought bold personality to her client’s family home.

A room painted in rich eggplant isn’t exactly a subtle introduction. But in this Wilmette, Illinois, home, that’s precisely the point. Just beyond the foyer, a dining room drenched in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal, complete with a glossy lacquered ceiling, offers the first sign that the designer at the wheel had little interest in playing it safe. And, as Kate Haynes of Noble Studio quickly discovered, neither did her client.

The pair connected over Instagram as construction on the family’s 6,500-square-foot home neared completion. With the architectural details and hard finishes already in place, Haynes was tasked with giving the new build some personality. Luckily, the homeowner, a former graphic designer, had plenty to spare. “We talked about incorporating deeply saturated colors along with a lot of personal touches,” recalls Haynes. “She is one of the warmest, most delightful people, and her home totally reflects her personality.”

What Haynes created for the family is a masterclass in exuberance, informed in part by the homeowner’s Pakistani heritage. Rather than lean into overt cultural references, Haynes borrowed from the richness of Mughal architecture and the bold, jewel-toned palettes of South Asia, weaving both together throughout the five-bedroom home. In the daughter’s bedroom, a scalloped headboard recalls a multifoil arch; elsewhere, vintage rugs lend the new construction a sense of history. “We wanted to have that original depth that can only come from a textile that has seen some life and years,” Haynes explains.

There’s plenty of levity, too: a hanging chair in the blue reading room, a rock wall in the playroom, and a custom circular banquette that rivals the best restaurant in town. Each detail speaks to a family that takes its pleasures seriously, whether that’s gathering with friends for dinner or curling up with a book. “She was just a person who looks for things to be delighted by,” Haynes says of her client. Consider the house a reflection of that philosophy.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Noble StudioBuilder: Sterling Wilson DevelopmentLocation: Wilmette, ILThe Space: A five-bedroom, seven-bathroom home across 6,500 square feet

An expansive crane mural sets a serene, transportive mood in the primary suite, where “subtle eastern influence and massive graphic appeal” establish an earthy palette, says Haynes. The blush pink upholstered bed is flanked by khaki green Lawson-Fenning nightstands, while a rustic vintage Indian teak bench adds a timeworn counterpoint to the room'så more refined furnishings.

Cornflower blue wainscoting and patterned Penny Morrison wallpaper establish a cheerful backdrop in the daughter’s bedroom, where saffron yellow accents tie the space into the rest of the home’s palette. “The homeowner had seen an inspirational image with geometric wainscoting topped with a patterned wallpaper, and it really resonated with her, so she asked us to run with that idea,” adds Haynes.

“We wanted to create serene escapes in each of the two guest rooms that we worked on,” explains Haynes of this enveloping escape, swathed in a taupey pink. “We loved the Penny Morrison block printed graphic fabric that is on the drapes—the coppery brown is so rich against the quieter wall.”

“Our client is an avid reader, and she had wanted to create a dreamy blue space with a hanging chair where she can eventually cultivate plants that will become a living, breathing part of this space,” says Haynes. A Murphy bed is built into the bookshelves for additional sleeping space when the family has a full house, while the graphic sofa plays into the client’s adventurous side.

Designed to keep the couple’s daughter entertained through Chicago’s long winters, the playroom is equal parts imagination and movement. A modular foam sofa can be reconfigured into a fort or gymnastics landing pad, while a custom climbing wall encourages active play. Haynes’s favorite detail? A hand-crocheted saffron yellow swing that brings the home’s recurring signature hue into its most playful space.

Drama was on the menu in the dining room, where walls painted in Farrow & Ball’s Brinjal meet a matching lacquered ceiling. Getting the glossy aubergine finish just right took several attempts, but the result was worth the trouble.

In the home’s soaring living room, Haynes tempered the airy architecture with a collection of tactile, earthy furnishings. Seafoam sofas dressed in block-printed pillows mingle with woven armchairs, a pair of travertine coffee tables, and a vintage African stool. The mix lends visual weight to the expansive space without sacrificing the comfortable, family-friendly atmosphere.

A deep archway visible from the front door offered the perfect opportunity for a little surprise. Haynes tucked a pair of custom banquettes around a circular dining table, upholstering the snug seating in a saffron-and-blue block print sourced from Etsy. The palette echoes the nearby reading room, while the clever configuration makes everyday meals feel wonderfully intimate.

Founded originally in 2018 as Kate Haynes Interiors and reborn in 2023 as Noble Studio, Kate Haynes has been honing a fascination with spaces, architecture, and art since childhood. Her company has always been about beautiful spaces (naturally), but the rebirth as Noble Studio comes with a sense of mature intentionality to create quiet luxury for clients. Kate brings to her team a relentless commitment to the intangible result of joy present in the aesthetics and flow of a place, no matter what style it wears.

#6977. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

#6975. Does The Pitt Need More Romance?

Amy Poehler told Noah Wyle on her podcast that the actor should add a little steam to the medical drama. We agree.

Want to know one of the reasons why shows like ER and Grey’s Anatomy were such huge hits? Romance. People love to see people kissing! It’s hot! But The Pitt, a serious drama on HBO praised for its handling of grief and real-life healthcare problems, still only toys with the idea that their doctors could smooch.

That hasn’t stopped fans from setting characters up together. But when Amy Poehler directly asked The Pitt star Noah Wyle about the lack of romance, the four-time Emmy winner—who came on Poehler’s podcast Good Hang to promote that he’s People’s “Sexiest Man Alive” this year—responded that they’re more concerned with putting together a realistic medical drama.

“If you're gonna show so much guts and blood, if you're gonna go there, which you go there, and it's incredible, your makeup team is incredible, the stuff you do is bananas ... but if you're gonna show that much, every time you do that, you got to get a little smooch,” Poehler suggested. “Someone's got to do a little smooch.”

When Wyle joked that the blood-to-romance ratio could be “four-to-one, but certainly not one-to-one,” Poehler insisted, “You’re gonna show the guts, you gotta show some butts.” Wyle fired back by saying the show does have naked butts, but Poehler argued it should be steamier.

Wyle conceded: “We don’t show those kinds of butts.”

To be honest, Poehler is onto something. In contrast to Wyle’s previous hospital drama ER, which was chock-full of romantic subplots, The Pitt is devoid of that sort of soap opera business. While that’s allowed The Pitt to be a “serious” show about the grave responsibility of emergency doctors and frontline healthcare workers, it’s still television. This is an entertainment medium built on spectacle and fantasies. People are lonelier than ever, with men and women opting out of dating. We crave touch!

Sure, we might tune in to The Pitt anyway. But while we’re here, why can’t we also see Dr. Mohan (Supriya Ganesh) and Dr. Abbott (Shawn Hatosy) get a little frisky? Dr. Robby (Wyle) clearly had a situationship brewing with case manager Noelle Hastings (Meta Golding) in season 2. What happened there?

Production on The Pitt season 3 is set to begin this very week in Pittsburgh, so it may be too late for the show’s writers to incorporate Poehler’s (correct) note about kissing and butts. But maybe we can hope that the creators have heard the legion of very outspoken Pitt fans and wrote in a few treats for those who live and die by their favorite “ships” from the show. If you don’t know what that means, trust me when I say it will probably never influence how you already watch The Pitt.

#6974. I Finally Found a Reclining Sofa That Doesn’t Look Like a Reclining Sofa—and I'm Never Going Back

It has held up against kids and the family cat, too.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last time I had a recliner in my house was in 1999—a polyester-blend, marshmallow-like sectional that had a side lever to pop out a footrest. Stylish it was not, but most of my friends had something similar in their parents’ house. That’s one of the reasons I resisted even looking at recliners for almost 30 years. That is until I noticed a wave of modern variations with a stealth reclining option.

My regular sectional suddenly felt like a dud. It had a chaise and an ottoman, but whoever sat in the middle had to prop their feet on the coffee table, which is far from comfy. Plus, it looked a bit bulky in my sunroom, which doubles as a family hangout spot and movie night hub.

When I saw the POVISON Elara reclining sectional, I was smitten. I could customize it to have three recliners—a win for my family of four. I ordered it in a silvery gray upholstery, which added an airy vibe to my sunroom. Since the carpet underneath is light blue, I needed some depth. I accessorized it with woodsy hues via the geometric Frank Lloyd Wright print pillows from Faribault Mill and a Boll & Branch grand waffle Marino wool blanket. The look was something straight out of House Beautiful pages, IMHO.

I’ve had the POVISON for over three months now, and it has become my family’s favorite furniture piece in the house. Read my full review of it ahead.

Best For: This sectional is all about TLC and suits anyone who wants to sink into a sofa and put their feet up, quite literally. Great for families and roommates.

Not ideal for: Very small spaces. While you can order a two-seater sofa that is 83 inches long, the armrest is 43.5 inches wide, and that’s before you extend the foot recliner.

Price at review (3 armless chairs and 2 armrests): $3,699

Bottom Line: The smooth reclining technology of the Elara sectional makes relaxing easy, but it folds up into a sleek silhouette. Since there are no loose cushions or seating, you don’t have to worry about sagging or fluffing.

While front-door delivery is free, I opted for White Glove delivery, which is $119. Two people delivered four boxes—three to accommodate each seat and one for the arms of the sofa. Each piece was wrapped in a quilted “baggie” that was definitely a step up from the flimsier covers of other boxed furniture that had shipped to my house. Because these are on par with storage seat covers, I kept them and plan to use them to store my outdoor seating in the basement during winter.

The team unpacked all the boxes and assembled the pieces, making sure that the wiring worked properly before leaving. Each piece needs to be plugged in, so I had to get an extension cord. They took away the boxes, and the entire delivery/assembly process took less than 30 minutes.

My kids volunteered to push all the buttons to make sure the recliner sections worked. I was a little anxious about all the back and forth as they figured out the ideal reclining position, but my sofa comes with a two-year warranty, so that puts me at ease. (New orders qualify for a limited five-year warranty.)

If someone wants to recline in the middle seat they can.

The headrest is adjustable too to your desired position.

When asked how this sectional feels, the word that pops into my head is “super.” When I watch movies, I can tweak the seating just so. I don’t like to lean back all the way, so I like that the headrest can be more vertical and support my neck, but my legs can be fully extended.

The high-resilience cushions are described as having a “cloud bounce,” and I would agree. They are very soft, but not so much that I feel like the couch is going to swallow me up. There is a nice give that is still comfortable, not too firm. Because the upholstery is chenille, it is soft to the touch, and this only adds to the luxurious lounging moment.

When the head portion is not facing up, it folds down to a platform-like top that my cat adores. He will sit on it like it is a throne and look out of the windows. This is also important to me because my sunroom has two big picture windows, a selling point, and I would be devastated if they were blocked by a sofa headrest. I like that the Elara sits low.

That said, I, too, look out the window and sit on the sofa with my legs stretched out across the seats. I use two pillows to give my back more support as I rest against the arm.

While this is not a sleeper sofa, one person can definitely sleep or nap on the Elara. I wasn’t planning on spending the night on the sofa, but when I was tossing and turning in my bed and needed a change of scenery to fight my insomnia, this was the spot. I used one of the three included lumbar pillows and a weighted Luxome blanket and slept soundly until the morning. I’d do it again in a heartbeat.

When my father-in-law was visiting, he reclined all the way and took more than one nap with zero complaints.

I ordered the Elara in a Mist gray colorway, which is a bit lighter than I anticipated. It looks beautiful, and I want to keep it that way. I have a strict no eating on the sectional policy. We are a no-shoes-indoors family, but even so, I hound my kids about keeping their feet and hands clean. (And no one is allowed on here with their soccer socks!) My family may think I am a bit of a nuisance, but I just really want to keep the sofa from getting streaky marks or stains. The performance fabric is supposed to be water resistant, but I don’t want to take any chances.

My cat has not scratched at it yet, but there was one section where he stretched with his claws out and pulled out a thread. I snipped it off, and no one can tell. Three months into ownership, and the sectional looks the same as it did the day it was delivered.

I would definitely recommend the POVISON Elara sectional to anyone who wants to up their TV nights and R&R. When I am on the sofa, it reminds me of those fancy movie theater seats, but because the upholstery is so soft and cozy, I feel like I am in a spa.

My entire family (and cat) love this sectional. It is hands down a welcome addition to the house. Because it is a recliner, you can tuck the headrest and the footrest away, so the silhouette looks sleek and actually takes up less space than my old sectional with its chaise and ottoman. The only catch is if you have a heavy coffee table, it might be annoying having to move it out to make room for the foot extender.

The arms are already wide, which works great for resting, but I also added the Apto side table to create a little station for books, and I don’t need to worry about having anything loose on the coffee table. This combo makes the sectional function like an island where we could all gather and relax.

#6973. This Designer’s Long Island Family Home Has Been 40 Years in the Making

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat is a laboratory for color, collecting, and the occasional improbable antique.

Phillip Thomas’s first shop had a captive clientele. As a child, he commandeered the mudroom of his family’s house in Bellport, New York, filled it with yard-sale finds he had repainted or refinished, and ushered his parents’ friends inside. He called the enterprise Phillip & Co. (“There was no Co.,” he says. It was just him.) His customers, he recalls, were more or less obligated to buy something.

The Phillip & Co. space is now a pantry for extra tableware, but Thomas is still rearranging the house around it. His family has owned the late-19th-century home for 40 years, and the New York–based interior designer returns almost every weekend. The four-bedroom house includes two porches, a sunny sitting room overlooking the bay, and a living room anchored by a large fireplace. Thomas still sleeps in his childhood bedroom when there, and his parents, siblings, nieces, and friends gather at the property regularly, too.

Thomas’s Manhattan apartment is white and contemporary, filled with Latin American art. Bellport called for something different: “an American cottage by the sea,” he says. “I really just want to feel comfortable and I want everyone around me to feel at ease.” Getting there has involved decades of experimentation, a few unlikely purchases, and regular consultation with a formidable client committee: his family. “It’s a democracy,” he says. “It’s not a dictatorship.”

His love of Bellport extends well beyond the borders of his family’s property. The South Shore village has long been a mecca for creatives. The South Bay Art Association held its first official meeting at the village’s community center in 1957. Thomas, who has been coming to Bellport since he was four, remembers spending time in that same building as a child. In 2019, he led the renovation of its basement bowling alley and recreation space through the Bellport Village Program Fund.

There, he took Long Island’s Great South Bay as a starting point, introducing blue floors punctuated with red circles that evoked buoys, which village workers helped paint. The project restored a childhood gathering place for another generation, with color doing much of the work on a limited budget. It is a public expression of the instincts that have shaped his family’s house: keep what matters, reconsider what no longer works, and make the result inviting enough that people will use it.

At home, those decisions are rarely final. The dining room has gone from lacquered hunter green to red to its current painted pattern, where undulating lines surround a large, exuberantly colored painting. Elsewhere, Thomas used stencils to give walls the irregularity he seeks out in handmade things. “Having something painted onto the walls gives the room soul,” he says. “There is perfection in imperfection.”

The collections have accumulated on similar terms. Antique tools, gathered with his father over decades, line a hallway in carefully ordered arrangements. Thomas admires them as objects made by hand to facilitate other handmade things; displayed together, their worn wooden forms are sculptural. In the powder room, a coal-mining cart found a second career as a basin, paired with a vintage mirror frame fitted with glass cut to accommodate the plumbing. “You’d be surprised what I’ll put in the back of my car,” he says.

Other pieces traveled even farther. A campy painting of dogs playing poker came with his mother’s family from Yugoslavia to northern Chile, then to Santiago. After his grandmother died, the family agreed it belonged in Bellport, where it hangs in Thomas’s studio. The family’s routines shift with the seasons, which in turn shape how they use the house. Winter draws everyone toward the living-room fireplace, where the sofa is covered in a Schumacher fabric reminiscent of an American rag rug and throw pillows are made from textiles Thomas found in Turkey. From May through October, life moves out to the porch, where varied flexible seating facilitates long dinners and several conversations at once.

For the porch fireplace, Thomas collaborated with local artist Jay Sylvester on a sculpture of foraged branches surrounding a bull’s-eye mirror. The reflection ensures that even someone seated with their back to the bay gets a view.

Even the garden has benefited from his instinct to collect. Thomas acquired Chilean sculptures, including a Corten-steel piece inspired by wind-gnarled trees. For the latest overhaul, he collaborated with John Beitel of Landspace Inc., who has tended the gardens for 35 years. Together, they shaped a landscape where formal beds give way to looser planting along paths that frame views of the water, with red dogwood branches providing color through winter.

For all its evolution, the house’s greater purpose has remained remarkably consistent. Thomas’s nieces now invite their own friends; bedrooms are invariably full on summer weekends; and everyone helps cook or set the table. His mother, Sava, spends time on the porch before the household wakes, with a cup of coffee and the breeze coming off the water. After 40 years, she is still photographing the view. “I take photos and photos and they’re all the same,” she says. “But it’s beautiful.”

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6972. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6971. It’s Time to Buy an Art TV. These Are the 4 Best Options.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are slim, glare-free, and give your house an extra touch of class.

Whether you call it an art TV, a frame TV, or a gallery TV, we’re all talking about the same thing. An anti-reflective matte screen, paired with a thin (often wood or wood-looking) bezel so that when it’s turned off you can approximate a gallery painting or photographs instead of staring at a blank black screen. These trendy TVs are the new best way for home design lovers to not compromise their aesthetic visions while still entertaining. Art TVs are for the people who care about their home design as much as their actual TV watching experience.

The breakout, genre-defining example was The Frame. Samsung’s 2017 release was the first TV model that truly masqueraded as an art piece on your wall. Instead of a big blank space on your wall, you now have art when you're not watching TV, and a TV when you are. Got a date coming over? Switch that black screen to one of any classic artworks from The Met or one of many museums worldwide. It took a few iterations to get right, but less than a decade later Samsung was doing enough right that other TV manufacturers, Hisense, LG, and TCL are following suit.

These four art TVs are a marvelous blend of form and function. I tried to pick a range of sizes between 50 and 75 inches, but most of these TVs come in every size between 55 inches and 85 inches. Whatever your budget or space allows, you’ll find the right one here.

Samsung still makes the best art TV out there. With the introduction of the Frame Pro line, Samsung made the first premium art TV. Premium picture quality, premium sound (for once), and—if you’re willing to pay a handsome $4.99 a month—the largest selection of fine art you can get on one of these. Even for the low price, it’s annoying to essential pay the Met a subscription fee to use one of the main features of your TV. But, here’s the upside. It’s all real art and no AI; refreshing in this economy. The free library cycles through about 30 new pieces of art a month, so even if you aren’t paying you’re getting a new influx of (real) art periodically.

Free with your package is Samsung's One Connect Box. This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics. It opens you up to put your Frame anywhere the TV fits on your wall, while your cable box and gaming consoles are plugged into the OCB and stowed out of sight. The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.

Compared to the more ubiquitous Google TV experience you’ll get on the Hisense and TCL, Samsung’s user interface takes some getting used to if you haven’t driven one of these TVs before. Even with familiarity, it’s still not my favorite to navigate.

The Frame Pro might be the flagship, but Samsung is still updating its regular Frame model each year with great success. This 2026 update to the original Frame is a slightly more affordable, slightly less impressive version of what you get with the Frame Pro. It’s equipped with everything Samsung has figured out over the years: an anti-reflective matte display, options for customizable bezels, and a massive accumulated gallery of artwork to choose from.

It may not technically reach the heights of the Pro, but the picture quality is super clear and the colors are vivd enough for most viewers to be satisfied. On the whole, it has a lot of the same pros (and all the same cons) as the beefier Pro for a few hundred dollars cheaper.

If you need something smaller than 55-inches, this is the only art TV that clears the bar. Luckily, it is a very good one. The 2026 Hisense Canvas looks and sounds fantastic, the setup is way more streamlined than The Frame, and it runs Google TV, which has become my OS of choice due to the consistent performance and easy setup. In my team’s tests, we found this to be the best looking art TV for the money, even if it technically fell short of the Frame Pro.

The best part of the Hisense art TV experience is that you don't have to pay for the art. Not once, not ever. Plus, the CanvasTV even offers a 144 Hz refresh rate mode for gaming with low latency. Not that I’d recommend gaming on an art TV, but if it’s your only option, Hisense makes it at least tolerable. We only wish we had the convenience of the Samsung OCB with the free art offerings of the CanvasTV. Now, that would be heaven.

Dollars to donuts, this 75-inch TCL art TV is the best value in the category, costing roughly as much as a 55-inch Samsung Frame Pro. The budget-friendly display comes at the cost of performance and color. It's got muddier blacks that can make night scenes hard to watch and it's not as vibrant as The Frame or the CanvasTV.

For one, this TV does have one advantage that helps it outweigh the others; it's the only frame TV that comes close to being as thin as an actual piece of artwork. It only has one bezel option, but at least it’s a relatively neutral off-white.

The art selection is decent, and we like how easy the TV and Google OS makes it to share your own photos instead. TCL also boasts about the hundreds of thousands of pieces of AI artwork you can access and create. It solves the lack of content problem, but a computer plagiarizing Van Gogh is never going to be a fraction as beautiful as a picture of an actual Van Gogh.

In short, art TVs are meant to be able to double as framed artwork when they are not in use. The features of these TVs include:

For me, it’s got to be 65-inches. Even in my (relatively) small Brooklyn apartment, this is the sweet spot for TV size. Anything larger is really just for shits and giggles at that point. 55 inches is generally the smallest (aside from Hisense with its 50 inch) size of art TV you can get and it’s largely pretty acceptable, but know if you have a large wall that even that may feel small.

The OS is basically the whole look and feel of a smart TV interface. Roku City is comforting, but you won't get that here. The only options are Google TV or Samusng's Tizen OS. Between the Tizen interface and Google TV, we give the slight edge to the Google UI and OS. It's smoother and gives more options for integrating Google Photos—a great feature to have on an art TV. As far as TV operating systems go, though, they are pretty similar in terms of layout and amount of space dedicated to advertising.

We've done the hard work for you by spending years watching these TVs up close. We've seen them in action on showroom floors, in private demo sessions, and (most important of all) in our homes. We've awarded the best of the best. Needless to say, we did all the heavy lifting for you. Sometimes literally.

#6970. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

#6966. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

#6963. Frank Lloyd Wright’s Only Planetarium Reopens in Florida After 6-Year Restoration

The Florida Southern College landmark is open for the first time in more than two decades.

The only planetarium that Frank Lloyd Wright ever built shut down in 2004, more than four decades after the famous American architect completed the dome-topped structure on the grounds of Florida Southern College (FSC). The projector died, according to the Lakeland-based school, rendering the building functionally obsolete.

Now, the entire planetarium—with a new, state-of-the-art projector—just emerged from a six-year restoration and is once again welcoming students and the public.

Led by campus architect Jeff Baker of MCWB Architects, the long-awaited project is part of a larger effort by FSC to revive all 13 of its buildings by Wright, who conceived a master plan called “Child of the Sun” for the Lakeland college between 1938 and 1958. Complete with academic buildings, esplanades, chapels, and a library, the 113-acre campus is home to the largest collection of Wright-designed buildings anywhere in the world. In 2012, the National Park Service granted National Historic Landmark status to the school.

The two-phase renovation of the planetarium, which is located in the Polk Science Building, began in 2021 with the exterior. The team updated the roof, flashing (weatherproofing), and stucco cladding, and replaced 280 custom textile-block tiles on the facade. Wright developed his signature textile-block system in the 1920s, applying geometric patterns to hand-cast concrete tiles and then sheathing both exteriors and interiors of his buildings to create a tactile, woven look. The planetarium’s tiles are among the architect’s most straightforward, defined by simple horizontal bands and a vertical “stitching” motif.

By 2023, the project moved indoors. The main objective with the interior was to restore Wright’s original vision, which had been augmented through the years with awkward additions, like a computer closet from the 1980s.

Now, formerly deep-blue walls are a calming neutral hue, while the flooring is back to “Cherokee Red,” another Wright signature found throughout many of his Usonian designs. Local artisans also collaborated on restoring the structure’s 60-seat theater, reviving the original circular floor plan with a seating system crafted out of plywood and red vinyl upholstery.

Capping off the project is the addition of a new Cosm projector and Bowens sound system, both of which provide state-of-the-art functionality befitting a modern planetarium. Perhaps best of all, it’s not just FSC students who will benefit: The public can experience the planetarium as intended through a series of four rotating shows each week. Among the scheduled offerings is “Destination Mars: The New Frontier,” which focuses on humanity’s epic endeavors to the Red Planet.

“Reopening the planetarium allows us to bring an important part of Florida Southern’s history back to life while creating new opportunities for our students and the community,” said Tory King, director of tourism operations and visitor experience at FSC, in a statement. “The architectural restoration brought the space back, and the new technology now allows us to return it to active educational and public use.”

Tickets are $20 per person and $15 per person for families and groups of four or more.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6962. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6961. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.

#6957. This Heritage Pattern Is Replacing Flat, Beige Walls—Here’s How Designers Are Using It in 2026

The centuries-old motif also makes lampshades, rugs, and seating feel bespoke.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In an age of encroaching AI, designers are turning to heritage looks to create bespoke interiors with personality. That may be one reason decorative marbling is having a moment.

"People are tired of flat, safe walls,” says designer Cara Woodhouse. “After years of greige, clients want surfaces with a point of view, and marbling has an artisanal history behind it. It's also flexible: it can read painterly, graphic, or soft depending on the colorway."

Its versatility is part of the appeal. “It doesn't necessarily have to be traditional or even executed on paper,” adds designer Sasha Bikoff. “It's really about the movement of color and the organic, unpredictable patterns that emerge, which can translate beautifully across wallpaper, furniture, textiles, and decorative objects.”

The paper marbling technique has a long history. In 10th-century Japan, artisans created “floating ink” designs by placing ink on water and manipulating it as it moved. By the 15th century, the practice was known in Turkey as “the art of clouds,” before making its way to Venetian and Florentine bookbinders.

The painterly look is appealing because its undulating lines feel expressive and vibrant without harsh edges, says Jo Plant, chief creative officer at Pooky, which is a brand well-known for its marbled lampshades.

Designers are bringing marbling into all kinds of interiors, from murals and upholstery to lampshades and even sink skirts. Analisse Taft-Gersten, cofounder of the 1818 Collective, used billowy marbled silk to elevate a powder room. And studios like Rule of Three are experimenting with the technique in floral fabrics that resemble vines.

Should you need further convincing, Woodhouse puts it best: "Marbling is pattern without repetition. No two passes are the same, so a wall reads more like art than wallpaper. It brings depth and a handmade quality to a room that might otherwise feel too finished."

Here are nine designer-approved ways to bring marbled motifs into your home.

Designer Michelle Fahmy of Haus of Meeshie likes things cheeky and playful. So it was a no-brainer to introduce groovy swirls into a Culver City pied-à-terre via a large-scale mural from Mitchell Black.

“I wanted something dramatic for the stairwell wall which bleeds into the living room and is the first thing you see when you walk in the door,” she says. “When I came across this wallpaper, it stopped me in my tracks! Usually, you see this paper marbling technique done on fabric or in smaller applications, like a lampshade, so this was unusual in scale and exactly the conversation starter I was looking for.”

For Bikoff, interior design should feel like a natural extension of a home’s architectural language. To bring her own sense of color, movement, and personality into a project within a Zaha Hadid building, she opted for Pierre Frey wallpaper with a liquid, dripping marble effect rather than a traditional, tightly swirled pattern.

“I love how the elongated, flowing lines create a sense of movement, almost as though the walls are melting or the colors are cascading down the surface,” Bikoff says. “Traditional marbling can feel very classical, whereas this interpretation feels more contemporary, psychedelic, and expressive.”

The dialogue between the wallpaper and Hadid's architecture is all about challenging conventional forms and embracing organic movement. “I wanted the room to feel immersive, almost like stepping inside a piece of abstract art,” she adds.

It was love at first sight. Marta St. Amour, interior designer and project manager at Braswell Design + Build was enchanted by the beautiful movement and layered tones of amber, soft blue, and sandy beige portrayed in the Lee Jofa Taplow wallpaper.

“The pattern became the inspiration for the bathroom's color palette, tying together the warm veining of Taj Mahal quartzite, rich caramel-colored shower tile, and soft blue tile behind the freestanding tub,” she says.

“We wanted the tub to feel like a statement piece, and the wallpaper's organic, flowing pattern complements its sculptural shape and warm metallic finish.”

To capture the spirit of the marbling technique but in a contemporary way, Bikoff opted for resin chairs with the telltale pattern in the above space.

“The way the colors blend and swirl through the resin gives them this incredible sense of depth and movement, almost like a liquid material that has been frozen in time,” she says.

Juxtaposing materials can challenge the perception of design. Here, the chairs feel like functional sculptures as they balance the visual richness of marble and the playful transparency of resin.

“They bring together color, texture, and fluidity while reinforcing the playful, futuristic spirit of the space."

If a full room of swirls is a bit much for you, settle on something smaller-scale, such as a lampshade. Pooky’s hand-marbled shades show off the dance of acrylic paint.

“Marbling is having a resurgence because we’re all craving craft, texture, and character in our homes,” says Plant. “People want pieces that feel human and handmade rather than mass-produced.” Adding a marbled shade is like decor candy that can add just the right amount of maximalism.

What makes the marbling technique unique is that the patterns are inherently organic, and so they lend themselves to pairing well with other organic shapes. Taft-Gersten anchored this powder room with a silk sink skirt featuring Rule of Three fabric that almost looks like it was kissed by bubbles.

“There’s a rhythm to it, but also an irregularity that keeps your eye moving; it never feels rigid,” Taft-Gersten says. The print also echoes the convex pebble mirrors above.

This girl’s bedroom may look dreamy, but that’s not what Cara Woodhouse was after when she landed on cotton candy-like Merenda Wallpaper by artist Michelle Weinberg.

“I wanted movement,” Woodhouse says. “A kid's room should feel alive, and marbled paper gives the walls energy without a single literal motif. I'd call it soft modern with an edge: rounded forms, blush tones, and a pattern that keeps it from going saccharine. The swirl does the work so the furniture can stay simple and sculptural."

The look of poured paint over a rug is one that will surely raise an eyebrow. Floor_Story designer Bekah Smith was experimenting with duct tape when she stumbled upon unexpected patterns that look like creases and shadows.

“There’s a similar sense of spontaneity to marbling, where patterns emerge through experimentation rather than being entirely predetermined,” Smith says.

For those looking for a ripple effect without the psychedelic swirls, consider ceramics as a way to dabble in the style without being so literal. Henry Holland Studio employs a traditional Japanese ceramic technique, nerikomi, to create irregular earthenware marbled motifs.

The pattern is created by stacking, folding, and rolling colored clay into slabs, then hand-molding them into functional shapes like lamp bases, bowls, and vases.
